I can tell myself, “They’re probably just busy,” but another part of me takes it as proof that everything is closing down—that my career is slipping away and people have already written me off. So yes, it’s everyday things too, but they get pulled into this larger worry almost immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think most of it still somehow ties back to the job loss or the divorce if you trace it far enough, but it doesn’t feel contained to just those two things anymore.\n\nMy mind jumps all over the place. I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like, not just “he’s between roles,” but like I’ve been marked somehow. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, even though logically I know one delayed email shouldn’t mean that.\n\nSo if you mean totally unrelated things, maybe not always totally unrelated. But in the moment it feels like everything becomes part of the same threat. It spreads.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people would feel after losing a job and going through a divorce, does your anxiety feel excessive or out of proportion?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is out of proportion, even if part of me keeps trying to justify it because the situation is genuinely hard.\n\nWhat makes me say that is how fast my mind escalates things. It’s not just, “I need to find contract work,” which would be normal. It turns into this whole chain where I’m thinking about contract work, then the mortgage, then whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter not answering an email quickly, can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know, rationally, a delayed email doesn’t mean that. People are busy. Things get missed. But emotionally it lands like confirmation that I’m finished professionally, or that everyone has already decided not to take me seriously anymore. So yes, compared with what I think most people would feel, it feels bigger and harder to shut off than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about things that are unrelated to your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… although I keep getting stuck on the word “unrelated,” because in my head everything somehow connects back to those two things eventually.\n\nBut it’s not like I only sit there calmly thinking, “I lost my job and I’m divorced.” It spreads. My mind keeps jumping from whether I’ll get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. Like there’s some quiet judgment happening behind the scenes.\n\nAnd then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to reply to an email, doesn’t stay minor. I start reading into it. I know logically it could mean nothing—they’re busy, they missed it, whatever—but it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. So even if the worries aren’t completely separate from the job loss or divorce, they take over everyday things in a way that feels much bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about things that have nothing to do with your job, divorce, money, or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot,” no. Not if you’re taking out job, divorce, money, and reputation—that’s… that’s most of what my head grabs onto right now.\n\nI mean, I probably worry about ordinary things too, like anyone does, but when I try to separate it out, it’s hard because everything seems to route back through those same lanes. If I’m worried about some random thing, I usually end up connecting it to whether I’m stable, whether I’m going to be okay, whether people see me differently now. So I don’t know if I have a clean example that has nothing to do with any of that.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a whole separate category of worries outside those things. It’s more that those things expand and sort of contaminate everything else.\n\nDoctor: If the job situation and divorce were fully resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel more contained, like there’s a clear switch that would turn off once the job and divorce pieces are handled. But honestly… I’m not sure it would mostly go away.\n\nI think it would probably quiet down some, because those are the big triggers. But the way my mind works right now, it keeps leaping from one thing to the next. It’s like I start with something concrete, like whether I’ll get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m imagining people in the Bethesda tech circles deciding I’m damaged goods or not worth taking seriously anymore.\n\nAnd what worries me is how little it takes to set that off. A recruiter doesn’t answer an email quickly, and instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind treats it like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down. So even if the divorce were settled and the job situation looked better, I’m afraid my brain would just find the next place to attach that same chain of worry.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about things that are unrelated to your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… although I keep getting stuck on the word “unrelated,” because in my head everything somehow connects back to those two things eventually.\n\nBut it’s not like I only sit there calmly thinking, “I lost my job and I’m divorced.” It spreads. My mind keeps jumping from whether I’ll get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. Like there’s some quiet judgment happening behind the scenes.\n\nAnd then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to reply to an email, doesn’t stay minor. I start reading into it. I know logically it could mean nothing—they’re busy, they missed it, whatever—but it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. So even if the worries aren’t completely separate from the job loss or divorce, they take over everyday things in a way that feels much bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about things that have nothing to do with your job, divorce, money, or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot,” no. Not if you’re taking out job, divorce, money, and reputation—that’s… that’s most of what my head grabs onto right now.\n\nI mean, I probably worry about ordinary things too, like anyone does, but when I try to separate it out, it’s hard because everything seems to route back through those same lanes. If I’m worried about some random thing, I usually end up connecting it to whether I’m stable, whether I’m going to be okay, whether people see me differently now. So I don’t know if I have a clean example that has nothing to do with any of that.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a whole separate category of worries outside those things. It’s more that those things expand and sort of contaminate everything else.\n\nDoctor: If the job situation and divorce were fully resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel more contained, like there’s a clear switch that would turn off once the job and divorce pieces are handled. But honestly… I’m not sure it would mostly go away.\n\nI think it would probably quiet down some, because those are the big triggers. But the way my mind works right now, it keeps leaping from one thing to the next. It’s like I start with something concrete, like whether I’ll get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m imagining people in the Bethesda tech circles deciding I’m damaged goods or not worth taking seriously anymore.\n\nAnd what worries me is how little it takes to set that off. A recruiter doesn’t answer an email quickly, and instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind treats it like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down. So even if the divorce were settled and the job situation looked better, I’m afraid my brain would just find the next place to attach that same chain of worry.\n\n[Last Question]\nBefore you lost your job, were you already a worrier like this?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 47 / computer_or_information_systems_manager / bachelors\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Duration\nmanifestation: \"This has been going on since I was fired about thirteen months ago, and it never really let up even after I found contract work.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through probably more than some people, but this level of worry really started when I was fired. That was about thirteen months ago now, and it’s like something shifted then.

And the frustrating part is, even after I got some contract work, it didn’t really settle down the way I thought it would. I kept thinking, “Okay, once I’m working again, I’ll calm down,” but it never fully let up. It just kept running in the background, like I couldn’t get back to the way I was before.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n19\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F43.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Yeah… thanks. I came in because I honestly feel like I can’t shut my brain off anymore. It’s like one thing starts it—something that should be manageable, like a client not responding to an email or going quiet after a call—and within minutes my mind has built this whole chain of disasters.\n\nI’ll think, okay, they’re unhappy, then I’m going to lose the contract, then I’m going to fall behind financially, especially with divorce payments, and then it turns into this bigger thing where I’m convinced I’m never going to get back to a real leadership role again. I know that sounds like a lot from one unanswered message, but in the moment it feels completely real and I can’t seem to stop it once it starts.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling this kind of excessive worry on most days for at least six months?\n\nPatient: Yes. Definitely longer than six months. It really started after I was fired, which was about thirteen months ago now, and I kept thinking it would settle down once I got some contract work and had something coming in again. But it didn’t really let up. It’s been… pretty constant since then, even with the contracts. Like the situation changed a little, but my head didn’t catch up with that at all.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever get sudden episodes of intense fear or physical panic that come on quickly, even when there isn’t an obvious immediate danger?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, exactly. I mean, I definitely get fear that ramps up and my body can feel on edge, but it usually feels tied to something I’m thinking about—like it has a storyline attached to it, if that makes sense.\n\nIt’s not usually like I’m just sitting there completely fine and then, out of nowhere, I’m hit with this sudden “I’m in danger” feeling for no reason. At least, I don’t think so. It’s more that my mind grabs onto something and then it escalates from there. But if you mean like a sudden panic attack, I’m not totally sure where the line is.\n\nDoctor: When you start worrying, is it hard for you to stop or control the worry?\n\nPatient: Yes. That’s honestly the part that scares me the most. Once it starts, I can see that it’s probably disproportionate, but I can’t make it stop.\n\nLike with the client example—if someone doesn’t respond to an email, I don’t just think, “They’re busy, they’ll get back to me.” I go straight into this whole chain where I’m checking my inbox, replaying the last conversation, wondering if I said something wrong, then suddenly I’m thinking I’m going to lose the contract, and if I lose the contract I won’t be able to keep up with the divorce payments, and then it turns into this bigger thing where I’m basically convinced I’ll never get back to a real leadership role again. It just keeps unfolding, one bad conclusion after another.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that sounds like a huge leap. But when I’m in it, it feels like I’m trying to argue with my own brain and losing. I’ll tell myself to stop, or that there’s no evidence yet, but it doesn’t really land. It’s like the worry has momentum once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about several different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just work, although work is sort of the center of gravity for it. It spreads. My mind jumps from the contract stuff to whether I’ll be able to keep up with the mortgage, and then from there to what people in the Bethesda tech circles think of me now. Like, do they see me as damaged goods because I got fired, or because I’m doing contract work instead of being in a leadership role?\n\nAnd it can start with something so small. A recruiter takes longer than usual to answer an email, and instead of thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” it feels like evidence that the whole door is closing. Like my career is narrowing in real time, and then I’m thinking about money, and the house, and my reputation, and whether people are quietly writing me off. It all links together really fast. So no, it’s not contained to one thing. It’s like everything becomes part of the same threat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. I mean, I’ve definitely felt worn down by all of this, and there are days where I feel discouraged or kind of heavy, like everything takes more effort than it should. But “down or depressed much of the time” feels harder for me to answer.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every day thinking, “I’m sad,” if that’s what you mean. A lot of the time I’m more tense or preoccupied than sad. But then after hours of worrying or feeling like I’m falling behind in life, I can feel pretty low. So… I guess I’m not sure where the line is between being depressed and just being exhausted by the stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think it’s like I’ve completely lost interest in things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always feel as present for them as I used to, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still do it, but part of me is distracted or kind of evaluating whether I should be doing something more productive. So I don’t know if that counts as losing pleasure, or if it’s just that my head is somewhere else. I can still enjoy things sometimes, but it can feel harder to settle into them.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been thinking of it as “nothing is enjoyable anymore.” It’s not that clear-cut for me. It’s more… the enjoyment gets interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been feeling restless or keyed up much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s honestly one of the more constant things. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is waiting for something to go wrong even when there’s nothing actually happening in that moment.\n\nI’ll notice my shoulders are up and locked, like I’m bracing without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll be on a perfectly ordinary call, and I’m bouncing my leg the whole time or shifting around in my chair like I can’t get comfortable. If I’m at home, I’ll pace from room to room, not even with a purpose half the time. I’ll get up, walk into the kitchen, then back to my office, then check something, then walk around again.\n\nIt’s that keyed-up feeling, like I’m ready for bad news. Like an email is going to come in, or the phone is going to ring, or something is about to drop. And even when nothing does, my body doesn’t really stand down. It’s tiring, but it’s also hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: During the last two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not most of the day nearly every day. I mean, I’ve had moments in the last couple weeks where I’ve felt pretty low or discouraged, but it doesn’t feel like a constant depressed mood all day.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down and then I’ll have stretches where I feel kind of bleak or heavy, but if you’re asking specifically whether I’ve been down or depressed for most of the day, almost every day, I’d say… probably no. At least not in the way I understand that question.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying ever latch onto things that aren’t connected to the job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it definitely centers around the job loss and the divorce a lot, but it doesn’t stay neatly inside those boxes. It sort of spreads.\n\nMy mind will jump from whether I’m going to get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are quietly thinking I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down, that people have already written me off.\n\nI know, logically, a delayed email can just be a delayed email. But in the moment it doesn’t feel that way. It feels connected to everything else, like one more sign that I’m not going to land on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is mainly about the job loss and divorce, rather than about lots of unrelated things?\n\nPatient: It started with those things, definitely—the job loss and the divorce are kind of the big engines underneath it. But it doesn’t stay that clean. It spreads into everything that’s even remotely connected, and then sometimes things that feel connected only because my mind makes them connected.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about whether I’m going to get enough contract work, and within a minute I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m damaged goods now. And then if a recruiter is slow to reply to an email, it doesn’t just feel like, “Oh, they’re busy.” It feels like proof that the whole thing is closing in—that my career is shutting down and people have already written me off.\n\nSo… yes, the job loss and divorce are at the center of it, but the worry doesn’t stay contained there. It branches out fast.\n\nDoctor: During the last two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had no interest or no pleasure most of the day nearly every day, if that’s the threshold you mean.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where things feel muted, or I’m distracted enough that I’m not really enjoying something the way I normally would. Like I might start doing something and realize my head isn’t really in it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been unable to feel interest or pleasure for most of the day, almost every day.\n\nSo… probably no, not in that persistent, all-day kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the job loss and divorce, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other everyday things too?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like I can keep it limited to, “Okay, I’m worried about the job and the divorce, and everything else is normal.” My mind doesn’t work that neatly right now.\n\nIt jumps around constantly. I’ll start with something practical, like whether I’m going to get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then that turns into wondering whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like they’re all quietly deciding I’m not someone worth taking a chance on anymore.\n\nAnd then something that should be small, like a recruiter not replying to an email quickly, feels much bigger than it should. I can tell myself, “They’re probably just busy,” but another part of me takes it as proof that everything is closing down—that my career is slipping away and people have already written me off. So yes, it’s everyday things too, but they get pulled into this larger worry almost immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think most of it still somehow ties back to the job loss or the divorce if you trace it far enough, but it doesn’t feel contained to just those two things anymore.\n\nMy mind jumps all over the place. I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like, not just “he’s between roles,” but like I’ve been marked somehow. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, even though logically I know one delayed email shouldn’t mean that.\n\nSo if you mean totally unrelated things, maybe not always totally unrelated. But in the moment it feels like everything becomes part of the same threat. It spreads.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people would feel after losing a job and going through a divorce, does your anxiety feel excessive or out of proportion?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is out of proportion, even if part of me keeps trying to justify it because the situation is genuinely hard.\n\nWhat makes me say that is how fast my mind escalates things. It’s not just, “I need to find contract work,” which would be normal. It turns into this whole chain where I’m thinking about contract work, then the mortgage, then whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter not answering an email quickly, can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know, rationally, a delayed email doesn’t mean that. People are busy. Things get missed. But emotionally it lands like confirmation that I’m finished professionally, or that everyone has already decided not to take me seriously anymore. So yes, compared with what I think most people would feel, it feels bigger and harder to shut off than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about things that are unrelated to your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… although I keep getting stuck on the word “unrelated,” because in my head everything somehow connects back to those two things eventually.\n\nBut it’s not like I only sit there calmly thinking, “I lost my job and I’m divorced.” It spreads. My mind keeps jumping from whether I’ll get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. Like there’s some quiet judgment happening behind the scenes.\n\nAnd then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to reply to an email, doesn’t stay minor. I start reading into it. I know logically it could mean nothing—they’re busy, they missed it, whatever—but it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. So even if the worries aren’t completely separate from the job loss or divorce, they take over everyday things in a way that feels much bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about things that have nothing to do with your job, divorce, money, or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot,” no. Not if you’re taking out job, divorce, money, and reputation—that’s… that’s most of what my head grabs onto right now.\n\nI mean, I probably worry about ordinary things too, like anyone does, but when I try to separate it out, it’s hard because everything seems to route back through those same lanes. If I’m worried about some random thing, I usually end up connecting it to whether I’m stable, whether I’m going to be okay, whether people see me differently now. So I don’t know if I have a clean example that has nothing to do with any of that.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a whole separate category of worries outside those things. It’s more that those things expand and sort of contaminate everything else.\n\nDoctor: If the job situation and divorce were fully resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel more contained, like there’s a clear switch that would turn off once the job and divorce pieces are handled. But honestly… I’m not sure it would mostly go away.\n\nI think it would probably quiet down some, because those are the big triggers. If I’m at home, I’ll pace from room to room, not even with a purpose half the time. I’ll get up, walk into the kitchen, then back to my office, then check something, then walk around again.\n\nIt’s that keyed-up feeling, like I’m ready for bad news. Like an email is going to come in, or the phone is going to ring, or something is about to drop. And even when nothing does, my body doesn’t really stand down. It’s tiring, but it’s also hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: During the last two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not most of the day nearly every day. I mean, I’ve had moments in the last couple weeks where I’ve felt pretty low or discouraged, but it doesn’t feel like a constant depressed mood all day.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down and then I’ll have stretches where I feel kind of bleak or heavy, but if you’re asking specifically whether I’ve been down or depressed for most of the day, almost every day, I’d say… probably no. At least not in the way I understand that question.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying ever latch onto things that aren’t connected to the job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it definitely centers around the job loss and the divorce a lot, but it doesn’t stay neatly inside those boxes. It sort of spreads.\n\nMy mind will jump from whether I’m going to get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are quietly thinking I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down, that people have already written me off.\n\nI know, logically, a delayed email can just be a delayed email. But in the moment it doesn’t feel that way. It feels connected to everything else, like one more sign that I’m not going to land on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is mainly about the job loss and divorce, rather than about lots of unrelated things?\n\nPatient: It started with those things, definitely—the job loss and the divorce are kind of the big engines underneath it. But it doesn’t stay that clean. It spreads into everything that’s even remotely connected, and then sometimes things that feel connected only because my mind makes them connected.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about whether I’m going to get enough contract work, and within a minute I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m damaged goods now. And then if a recruiter is slow to reply to an email, it doesn’t just feel like, “Oh, they’re busy.” It feels like proof that the whole thing is closing in—that my career is shutting down and people have already written me off.\n\nSo… yes, the job loss and divorce are at the center of it, but the worry doesn’t stay contained there. It branches out fast.\n\nDoctor: During the last two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had no interest or no pleasure most of the day nearly every day, if that’s the threshold you mean.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where things feel muted, or I’m distracted enough that I’m not really enjoying something the way I normally would. Like I might start doing something and realize my head isn’t really in it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been unable to feel interest or pleasure for most of the day, almost every day.\n\nSo… probably no, not in that persistent, all-day kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the job loss and divorce, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other everyday things too?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like I can keep it limited to, “Okay, I’m worried about the job and the divorce, and everything else is normal.” My mind doesn’t work that neatly right now.\n\nIt jumps around constantly. I’ll start with something practical, like whether I’m going to get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then that turns into wondering whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like they’re all quietly deciding I’m not someone worth taking a chance on anymore.\n\nAnd then something that should be small, like a recruiter not replying to an email quickly, feels much bigger than it should. I can tell myself, “They’re probably just busy,” but another part of me takes it as proof that everything is closing down—that my career is slipping away and people have already written me off. So yes, it’s everyday things too, but they get pulled into this larger worry almost immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think most of it still somehow ties back to the job loss or the divorce if you trace it far enough, but it doesn’t feel contained to just those two things anymore.\n\nMy mind jumps all over the place. I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like, not just “he’s between roles,” but like I’ve been marked somehow. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, even though logically I know one delayed email shouldn’t mean that.\n\nSo if you mean totally unrelated things, maybe not always totally unrelated. But in the moment it feels like everything becomes part of the same threat. It spreads.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people would feel after losing a job and going through a divorce, does your anxiety feel excessive or out of proportion?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is out of proportion, even if part of me keeps trying to justify it because the situation is genuinely hard.\n\nWhat makes me say that is how fast my mind escalates things. It’s not just, “I need to find contract work,” which would be normal. It turns into this whole chain where I’m thinking about contract work, then the mortgage, then whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter not answering an email quickly, can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know, rationally, a delayed email doesn’t mean that. People are busy. Things get missed. But emotionally it lands like confirmation that I’m finished professionally, or that everyone has already decided not to take me seriously anymore. So yes, compared with what I think most people would feel, it feels bigger and harder to shut off than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about things that are unrelated to your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… although I keep getting stuck on the word “unrelated,” because in my head everything somehow connects back to those two things eventually.\n\nBut it’s not like I only sit there calmly thinking, “I lost my job and I’m divorced.” It spreads. My mind keeps jumping from whether I’ll get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. Like there’s some quiet judgment happening behind the scenes.\n\nAnd then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to reply to an email, doesn’t stay minor. I start reading into it. I know logically it could mean nothing—they’re busy, they missed it, whatever—but it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. So even if the worries aren’t completely separate from the job loss or divorce, they take over everyday things in a way that feels much bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about things that have nothing to do with your job, divorce, money, or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot,” no. If I’m worried about some random thing, I usually end up connecting it to whether I’m stable, whether I’m going to be okay, whether people see me differently now. It’s more that those things expand and sort of contaminate everything else.\n\nDoctor: If the job situation and divorce were fully resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel more contained, like there’s a clear switch that would turn off once the job and divorce pieces are handled. But honestly… I’m not sure it would mostly go away.\n\nI think it would probably quiet down some, because those are the big triggers. But the way my mind works right now, it keeps leaping from one thing to the next. It’s like I start with something concrete, like whether I’ll get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m imagining people in the Bethesda tech circles deciding I’m damaged goods or not worth taking seriously anymore.\n\nAnd what worries me is how little it takes to set that off. A recruiter doesn’t answer an email quickly, and instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind treats it like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down. So even if the divorce were settled and the job situation looked better, I’m afraid my brain would just find the next place to attach that same chain of worry.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost your job, were you already a worrier like this?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. Yes, mostly. I mean, the timing is pretty clear to me: it started after I was fired, about thirteen months ago, and it just… never really stopped after that.

What bothers me is that I kept expecting it to ease up once I had something work-related again, even if it was just contract work. I thought, okay, once I’m not completely unemployed, once there’s some income and some structure, I’ll feel more like myself. But it didn’t really let up. It stayed there, kind of running underneath everything.

So yes, I do connect it to being fired. That feels like the point where everything changed. But it’s also gone on long enough now that it doesn’t feel like just a short reaction to one bad event. It’s been over a year of this, and even when circumstances improved a little, the anxiety didn’t really switch off.

It just keeps unfolding, one bad conclusion after another.\n\nAnd I know, sitting here, that sounds like a huge leap. But when I’m in it, it feels like I’m trying to argue with my own brain and losing. I’ll tell myself to stop, or that there’s no evidence yet, but it doesn’t really land. It’s like the worry has momentum once it gets going.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself worrying excessively about several different parts of your life, not just work?\n\nPatient: Yes, it’s definitely not just work, although work is sort of the center of gravity for it. So no, it’s not contained to one thing. It’s like everything becomes part of the same threat.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed much of the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it depressed, exactly. But “down or depressed much of the time” feels harder for me to answer.\n\nIt’s not like I wake up every day thinking, “I’m sad,” if that’s what you mean. A lot of the time I’m more tense or preoccupied than sad. But then after hours of worrying or feeling like I’m falling behind in life, I can feel pretty low. So… I guess I’m not sure where the line is between being depressed and just being exhausted by the stress.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest or pleasure in things you usually enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I don’t think it’s like I’ve completely lost interest in things, exactly. It’s more that I don’t always feel as present for them as I used to, if that makes sense.\n\nLike, if something is supposed to be enjoyable, I might still do it, but part of me is distracted or kind of evaluating whether I should be doing something more productive. So I don’t know if that counts as losing pleasure, or if it’s just that my head is somewhere else. I can still enjoy things sometimes, but it can feel harder to settle into them.\n\nI guess I haven’t really been thinking of it as “nothing is enjoyable anymore.” It’s not that clear-cut for me. It’s more… the enjoyment gets interrupted.\n\nDoctor: Over the past several months, have you been feeling restless or keyed up much of the time?\n\nPatient: Yes. Very much so. It’s honestly one of the more constant things. I feel wound up almost all day, like my body is waiting for something to go wrong even when there’s nothing actually happening in that moment.\n\nI’ll notice my shoulders are up and locked, like I’m bracing without realizing it. Sometimes I’ll be on a perfectly ordinary call, and I’m bouncing my leg the whole time or shifting around in my chair like I can’t get comfortable. If I’m at home, I’ll pace from room to room, not even with a purpose half the time. I’ll get up, walk into the kitchen, then back to my office, then check something, then walk around again.\n\nIt’s that keyed-up feeling, like I’m ready for bad news. Like an email is going to come in, or the phone is going to ring, or something is about to drop. And even when nothing does, my body doesn’t really stand down. It’s tiring, but it’s also hard to stop.\n\nDoctor: During the last two weeks, have you felt down or depressed most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so—not most of the day nearly every day. I mean, I’ve had moments in the last couple weeks where I’ve felt pretty low or discouraged, but it doesn’t feel like a constant depressed mood all day.\n\nIt’s more like I get worn down and then I’ll have stretches where I feel kind of bleak or heavy, but if you’re asking specifically whether I’ve been down or depressed for most of the day, almost every day, I’d say… probably no. At least not in the way I understand that question.\n\nDoctor: Does your worrying ever latch onto things that aren’t connected to the job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes. I mean, it definitely centers around the job loss and the divorce a lot, but it doesn’t stay neatly inside those boxes. It sort of spreads.\n\nMy mind will jump from whether I’m going to get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are quietly thinking I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter taking too long to respond to an email, can suddenly feel like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down, that people have already written me off.\n\nI know, logically, a delayed email can just be a delayed email. But in the moment it doesn’t feel that way. It feels connected to everything else, like one more sign that I’m not going to land on my feet.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel your worrying is mainly about the job loss and divorce, rather than about lots of unrelated things?\n\nPatient: It started with those things, definitely—the job loss and the divorce are kind of the big engines underneath it. But it doesn’t stay that clean. It spreads into everything that’s even remotely connected, and then sometimes things that feel connected only because my mind makes them connected.\n\nLike I’ll be thinking about whether I’m going to get enough contract work, and within a minute I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles are looking at me like I’m damaged goods now. And then if a recruiter is slow to reply to an email, it doesn’t just feel like, “Oh, they’re busy.” It feels like proof that the whole thing is closing in—that my career is shutting down and people have already written me off.\n\nSo… yes, the job loss and divorce are at the center of it, but the worry doesn’t stay contained there. It branches out fast.\n\nDoctor: During the last two weeks, have you had little interest or pleasure in doing things most of the day nearly every day?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve had no interest or no pleasure most of the day nearly every day, if that’s the threshold you mean.\n\nI mean, there are definitely times where things feel muted, or I’m distracted enough that I’m not really enjoying something the way I normally would. Like I might start doing something and realize my head isn’t really in it. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been unable to feel interest or pleasure for most of the day, almost every day.\n\nSo… probably no, not in that persistent, all-day kind of way.\n\nDoctor: Aside from the job loss and divorce, do you find yourself worrying a lot about other everyday things too?\n\nPatient: Yes, I do. It’s not like I can keep it limited to, “Okay, I’m worried about the job and the divorce, and everything else is normal.” My mind doesn’t work that neatly right now.\n\nIt jumps around constantly. I’ll start with something practical, like whether I’m going to get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about the mortgage, and then that turns into wondering whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like they’re all quietly deciding I’m not someone worth taking a chance on anymore.\n\nAnd then something that should be small, like a recruiter not replying to an email quickly, feels much bigger than it should. I can tell myself, “They’re probably just busy,” but another part of me takes it as proof that everything is closing down—that my career is slipping away and people have already written me off. So yes, it’s everyday things too, but they get pulled into this larger worry almost immediately.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry a lot about things that have nothing to do with your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think most of it still somehow ties back to the job loss or the divorce if you trace it far enough, but it doesn’t feel contained to just those two things anymore.\n\nMy mind jumps all over the place. I’ll be thinking about contract work, and then suddenly I’m on mortgage payments, and then I’m thinking about whether people in the Bethesda tech circles see me as damaged goods now. Like, not just “he’s between roles,” but like I’ve been marked somehow. And then if a recruiter takes too long to answer an email, it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down, even though logically I know one delayed email shouldn’t mean that.\n\nSo if you mean totally unrelated things, maybe not always totally unrelated. But in the moment it feels like everything becomes part of the same threat. It spreads.\n\nDoctor: Compared with what most people would feel after losing a job and going through a divorce, does your anxiety feel excessive or out of proportion?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it probably is out of proportion, even if part of me keeps trying to justify it because the situation is genuinely hard.\n\nWhat makes me say that is how fast my mind escalates things. It’s not just, “I need to find contract work,” which would be normal. It turns into this whole chain where I’m thinking about contract work, then the mortgage, then whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. And then something small, like a recruiter not answering an email quickly, can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down.\n\nI know, rationally, a delayed email doesn’t mean that. People are busy. Things get missed. But emotionally it lands like confirmation that I’m finished professionally, or that everyone has already decided not to take me seriously anymore. So yes, compared with what I think most people would feel, it feels bigger and harder to shut off than it should be.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself worrying a lot about things that are unrelated to your job loss or divorce?\n\nPatient: Yes… although I keep getting stuck on the word “unrelated,” because in my head everything somehow connects back to those two things eventually.\n\nBut it’s not like I only sit there calmly thinking, “I lost my job and I’m divorced.” It spreads. My mind keeps jumping from whether I’ll get enough contract work, to whether I can keep up with the mortgage, to whether people in the Bethesda tech circles think I’m damaged goods now. Like there’s some quiet judgment happening behind the scenes.\n\nAnd then something that should be minor, like a recruiter taking too long to reply to an email, doesn’t stay minor. I start reading into it. I know logically it could mean nothing—they’re busy, they missed it, whatever—but it can feel like proof that my whole career is closing down. So even if the worries aren’t completely separate from the job loss or divorce, they take over everyday things in a way that feels much bigger than it should.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying about things that have nothing to do with your job, divorce, money, or reputation?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say “a lot,” no. Not if you’re taking out job, divorce, money, and reputation—that’s… that’s most of what my head grabs onto right now.\n\nI mean, I probably worry about ordinary things too, like anyone does, but when I try to separate it out, it’s hard because everything seems to route back through those same lanes. If I’m worried about some random thing, I usually end up connecting it to whether I’m stable, whether I’m going to be okay, whether people see me differently now. So I don’t know if I have a clean example that has nothing to do with any of that.\n\nSo… I guess my answer is I haven’t really noticed a whole separate category of worries outside those things. It’s more that those things expand and sort of contaminate everything else.\n\nDoctor: If the job situation and divorce were fully resolved, do you think the worrying would mostly go away?\n\nPatient: I wish I could say yes, because that would make it feel more contained, like there’s a clear switch that would turn off once the job and divorce pieces are handled. But honestly… I’m not sure it would mostly go away.\n\nI think it would probably quiet down some, because those are the big triggers. But the way my mind works right now, it keeps leaping from one thing to the next. It’s like I start with something concrete, like whether I’ll get enough contract work, and then almost immediately I’m thinking about mortgage payments, and then I’m imagining people in the Bethesda tech circles deciding I’m damaged goods or not worth taking seriously anymore.\n\nAnd what worries me is how little it takes to set that off. A recruiter doesn’t answer an email quickly, and instead of just thinking, “Okay, they’re busy,” my mind treats it like evidence that the whole thing is collapsing—that my career is closing down. So even if the divorce were settled and the job situation looked better, I’m afraid my brain would just find the next place to attach that same chain of worry.\n\nDoctor: Before you lost your job, were you already a worrier like this?\n\nPatient: Not like this, no. I mean, I’ve always been someone who thinks things through probably more than some people, but this level of worry really started when I was fired. That was about thirteen months ago now, and it’s like something shifted then.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is, even after I got some contract work, it didn’t really settle down the way I thought it would. I kept thinking, “Okay, once I’m working again, I’ll calm down,” but it never fully let up. It just kept running in the background, like I couldn’t get back to the way I was before.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety feel mostly tied to what happened after you were fired?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I thought, okay, once I’m not completely unemployed, once there’s some income and some structure, I’ll feel more like myself. But it didn’t really let up. It stayed there, kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSo yes, I do connect it to being fired. That feels like the point where everything changed. It’s been over a year of this, and even when circumstances improved a little, the anxiety didn’t really switch off.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. That was about thirteen months ago now, and it’s like something shifted then.\n\nAnd the frustrating part is, even after I got some contract work, it didn’t really settle down the way I thought it would. I kept thinking, “Okay, once I’m working again, I’ll calm down,” but it never fully let up. It just kept running in the background, like I couldn’t get back to the way I was before.\n\nDoctor: Does your anxiety feel mostly tied to what happened after you were fired?\n\nPatient: Yes, mostly. I mean, the timing is pretty clear to me: it started after I was fired, about thirteen months ago, and it just… never really stopped after that.\n\nWhat bothers me is that I kept expecting it to ease up once I had something work-related again, even if it was just contract work. I thought, okay, once I’m not completely unemployed, once there’s some income and some structure, I’ll feel more like myself. But it didn’t really let up. It stayed there, kind of running underneath everything.\n\nSo yes, I do connect it to being fired. That feels like the point where everything changed. But it’s also gone on long enough now that it doesn’t feel like just a short reaction to one bad event. It’s been over a year of this, and even when circumstances improved a little, the anxiety didn’t really switch off."
